We now move to the 1st point of today's agenda: - Proposal for a National Strategy for Development Cooperation of the Republic of Croatia for the period from 2017 to 2021. The proposed act is the Government of the Republic of Croatia pursuant to Article 172. Rules of Procedure of the Croatian Parliament and on Article 6 of the Law on Development Cooperation and humanitarian aid and abroad. The debate was conducted by the Committee on Foreign Policy. I would like to ask the Prime Minister, the Deputy Prime Minister and the Minister of Foreign and European Affairs, the esteemed Maria Pejcinović-Buric to further explain the proposal. Here are the vice presidents and ministers.
ParlaMint-HR_2017-10-25-0.u54341	Thank you very much, Mr. President-in-Office of the Parliament, the esteemed and esteemed ladies and gentlemen of the Parliament. National strategy for the development cooperation of the Republic of Croatia for the period 2017/2021. the year has been prepared in accordance with the Law on Development Cooperation and humanitarian aid abroad. Upgrading the previous and first National Strategy of the Republic of Croatia in this way defines the national policy of international development cooperation and humanitarian assistance as one of the key components and important instruments of its foreign policy. International development policy is an instrument that helps developed countries in efforts to reduce poverty, strengthen institutions and boost economic growth. In foreign policy development cooperation can also be the first step in establishing more comprehensive, political, economic and social relations between developed and developing countries. Through development cooperation, we promote our own systems of value and political order and bring economic and social standards into line. As a result of such efforts, we open the door for political partnerships and support in international relations, we open up opportunities for economic cooperation and new markets for our companies and give our society new opportunities, knowledge, cooperation and exchange of knowledge and experience and technology. In just over 2 decades since its independence, the Republic of Croatia has passed the path from the country facing aggression, the Homeland War, the humanitarian crisis, we have been the recipient of development aid, we have passed the post-war reconstruction and recovery and democratic transition. By joining the NATO Alliance and the EU, we have joined a group of the most developed countries and also the largest and most influential donor in the world, which stands out more than half of the global development aid. It is important to note here that by increasing its gross national income in 2011, Croatia has exited the support group and has become an exclusive donor. The position of the Republic of Croatia in the global framework of development policy has been determined by the size, capacity and challenges of the new donor country. But also with the post-war transition experience recognised by the Republic of Croatia as its added value and with the help it will have the opportunity to contribute to the global development community by a proportion of relatively greater than the countries of similar external political influence and economic scope. Conscious of our comparative advantages but also limited resources and the fact that by encouraging the development of security and stability in other countries, we also ensure our own development, stability and security. In particular, through the framework of its development cooperation, the Republic of Croatia will seek to promote mutual interdependence and coherence between peace, security and development. In this context, the Republic of Croatia will advocate a comprehensive EU approach to transforming conflict into dialogue through early warning, conflict prevention, timely response to crises and their management, early recovery and sustainable recovery, stabilisation and peace building and state, and strengthening resilience to future crises and disasters and long-term sustainable development. When planning and implementing the policy of international, development and humanitarian assistance, the Republic of Croatia will continue to follow internationally established principles, paying particular attention to respect for the principle of solidarity, justice and humanity. Equally, development cooperation and humanitarian assistance will continue to be based on the principles of transparency, efficiency and orientation that will seek to further reduce dispersion and the increased impact of the aid granted. Finally, in order to ensure the full engagement of the partner country, we will insist on the mutual responsibility of the recipient at the beginning of the planning and implementation of the development programmes, which brings both partners out of which they are mutually empowered by new knowledge and experience. Accordingly, we will develop partnerships with international organisations that can most effectively respond to crisis situations. In line with the principle of policy coherence for development, the Republic of Croatia will further promote interinstitutional cooperation between the Ministry of Foreign and European Affairs as a national co-ordinator of international development and humanitarian policy, and institutions that have an impact on development and humanitarian policy. In fact, national policies such as trade, migration or security have a significant impact on development policy and changes in these policies. They may have a positive or negative impact on the progress of developing countries. The same number of ministries, offices and agencies in Croatia have knowledge and experience that directly conveys their own projects to interested developing countries. Therefore, as a platform for national coordination, an inter-resor working group on development cooperation and humanitarian assistance will continue to act as a platform for the exchange of views on the harmonisation and proposal of strategic decisions and guidelines. However, for the comprehensiveness of the action of national development and humanitarian policy, we also consider cooperation with extra-institutional partners such as the private sector, civil society. Among the scientific community teams, religious communities, etc. In this respect, we will continue active cooperation with both international and national civil society organisations under the Civil Society Platform for International Development Cooperation. Furthermore, through roof organisations and associations of economic operators and direct communication, the Republic of Croatia will strengthen cooperation with private sector stakeholders, with whom it will exchange experience and information on investment opportunities in developing countries, and consider new opportunities for cooperation in development projects such as the possibility of cooperation with the banking sector with regard to lending of such activities. Equally, co-operation with the scientific community will focus on developing and enriching educational programmes in the field of international development cooperation and humanitarian assistance, thus improving the knowledge and interest of this policy to the international scientific audience. Croatia has an advantage in international development policy as a rare and established donor with a recognised niche. More specifically, with its own experience of war and post-war democratic transition. This specifically means that we have our own practical knowledge, for example, of establishing a system of demining, development, resociation of defenders and the like. In addition, the recent role, the recent role of the recipient, allows us to better understand and respect the real needs of the recipient. These experiences have been sought and appreciated in the global community and are already being passed on to interested countries. However, as a new donor, we face certain challenges such as limited funding, legislative and administrative capacity. Taking into account the above facts in the National Strategy, we have chosen and set priorities for the Croatian development cooperation policy which are divided according to sectoral and geographical coverage. Sectoral priorities have been selected according to the criteria of efficiency of their own knowledge and experience in global frameworks, experience so far in implementation and existing capacities, focus on the main preconditions of development, and compliance with fundamental national priorities. In line with the above, Croatia will focus its development policy on three sectors. The first sectoral priority is the dignity of every human being. Croatian development cooperation and humanitarian assistance policy will be a guideline to human development, its dignity, rights and well-being, as well as the development of human abilities and the achievement of full potential regardless of gender, status, membership, belief or place of residence. The Republic of Croatia will target its development policy with the fight against poverty towards the ultimate goal of strengthening dignity and political, economic and social inclusion of individuals who will be satisfied with their lives and roles in society and thus contribute positively to the development of their environment. Special attention will be paid to education, health, and the protection and empowerment of women, children and young people. The second sectoral priority is peace and security, and the development of democratic institutions. A conscious of the far-reaching and long-term consequences of the conflict with the aim of reducing the differences of outbreak, spillover or restoration of conflict, the Republic of Croatia will point to the importance of timely recognition and action on the causes of the conflict. In this regard, we will focus on investigating the cause of the conflict, finding a suitable programme to contain them, and through scientific research and promoting the truth about the Homeland War, countries facing similar experiences, we will offer knowledge and experience in addressing challenges. In this regard, as a country with immigration and immigration experience, and especially as a country faced with the issue of refugees and exiles during the Homeland War, and as a member of the EU's external borders, the Republic of Croatia will continue to highlight the importance of finding quality programmes to combat the causes of migration at their source. We will stress the importance of effectively curbing all forms of radicalism through education on humanistic values such as respect for human life, equality of all people regardless of religious, national and other belongings, equality of men and women, education for peace, pluralism and tolerance. By respecting the local context in development cooperation, we will seek to offer experience and knowledge in the area of state building aimed at strengthening inclusive political and economic institutions that will promote the rule of law, proper governance and fundamental human rights. Compared to strengthening institutions, the emphasis will be placed on strengthening economic capacity, particularly strengthening small and medium-sized enterprises. Special attention will be paid to post-war transition, as well as to help EU accession, where we particularly consider important, Croatian experience to be passed on to candidate countries and potential candidates for EU membership, but also to countries covered by the European neighbourhood policy. The third sectoral priority is responsible economic development. Building sound foundations for the development of society depends on the establishment of a smart, sustainable and inclusive economic environment, which will be based on corporate, social and environmental responsibility, which is freed from corruption and which respects the local context. The Republic of Croatia will seek the economic empowerment of partner countries as an important boost to the sustainable and sustainable progress of a crisis-resistant society and natural disasters. As a country based on responsible use of resources, protection of natural and biodiversity conservation, the Republic of Croatia will install a component of environmental protection in development programmes, particularly with regard to responsible economy, tourism, agriculture and fisheries, sustainable production and consumption, and the protection of marine eco-systems, as a general public good. Accordingly, in cooperation with the private sector, opportunities will be considered to support the development of small and medium-sized entrepreneurship, social entrepreneurship, lifelong learning for entrepreneurship and financial literacy, as key skills of women's entrepreneurship, knowledge-based economies, environmental protection and revitalisation of the economy after the conflict, especially in terms of tourism. We will encourage creativity, innovation and exchange of knowledge and experiences. In the sectoral, national strategy, we also set out geographical priorities, which have been identified in accordance with the state's shown interest in co-operation, the usability of specific Croatian knowledge and experience, the present experience of cooperation, and the overall interest of the Republic of Croatia in strengthening overall cooperation. Therefore, the first geographical priority is certainly Southeast Europe, within which the Republic of Croatia will pay particular attention to project co-operation with the following countries, Albania, Montenegro, Kosovo, Macedonia and Serbia. As a programme country, I would especially like to single out BIH, historical links, immediate neighbourhood, high degree of association between societies, cultures and economies, and the role of Croatian people as one of the 3 constituent peoples, are key reasons why the BIH is among the main development priorities of the Republic of Croatia. The BIH is in the next EU neighbourhood and is a very important factor for the stability and final integration of the entire Southeast Europe into its membership. For this reason, the Republic of Croatia has a strong interest in supporting and helping the BIH in political, social and economic development that would bring it closer to EU membership. In this respect, the Republic of Croatia provides assistance in the field of education, health, culture, institutional reforms and harmonisation with EU legislation. We are helping to strengthen civil society, strengthen young people, restore and build and encourage small entrepreneurship for the social and development of the private sector. The support of the BIH is aimed at developing a whole society with an additional emphasis on the Croatian people as the main driver of systematic pro-European reform processes and guaranteeing the survival of the multiethnic BIH. In order to stop and make its development cooperation in BIH more effective and to best respond to the development needs of the BIH, the Republic of Croatia, will develop a multi-annual development programme to gather all development activities. The development programme will be developed, implemented and supervised in cooperation with the relevant authorities of the BIH. These will be in line with the EU's development efforts, as well as other donors active in the BiH. This will also open the possibility of development partnerships with leading donors in the country. Another geographical priority is the southern and eastern neighbourhood, which is followed after Southeast Europe by the next Croatian interest ring, which is of security, economic and political importance not only for the Republic of Croatia, but also for the EU. That area, because of its geographical location, is a bridge between the EU, Africa and Asia. In this respect, this region is an important part of the debate on security and migration issues, as well as a partner with high capacity to develop economic relations. Within the wider region of the southern and eastern neighbourhood, the Republic of Croatia will pay special attention to co-operation with the following countries, Egypt, Iraq, Lebanon and Syria. More extensive, programme co-operation in this region will focus Croatia on 2 programming countries. First one's Jordan from the south neighborhood. Jordan can be singled out as an example of political stability that has a positive impact on economic and social development. Jordan is a neighboring common destination for refugees from surrounding crisis areas. So far, development cooperation between the Republic of Croatia and Jordan in the area of peace and security has shown significant positive effects and strengthening cooperation, will bring benefits to both countries. The second programming state is Ukraine. Ukraine's geographical position makes it an important partner for co-operation between the political East and the West. It is about the EU about a significant country in terms of political and security instability, and the current political security and economic situation in Ukraine is known to the Republic of Macedonia. For this reason, the Republic of Croatia has already contributed to the reduction of the consequences of the conflict, particularly through the transfer of Croatian experience in the peaceful reintegration of knowledge and experience in joining the EU, and through the provision of humanitarian and development assistance including through projects to build an institutional and legislative framework for the management of care for the evicted and avoided persons of mine action and humanitarian demining, as well as psychosocial assistance to victims, protection of cultural treasures, training of doctors in the treatment of post-traumatic stress, and the rehabilitation of defenders and others. The third geographical priority is developing countries. Co-operation with these countries, the Republic of Croatia will have the opportunity to carry forward its own experiences directly but also to open up opportunities for further development of mutual cooperation on the political and economic agenda, fostering development cooperation with countries such as Afghanistan and Tanzania, and will help to expand development cooperation beyond the narrow regional sphere, and will ensure openness to countries that show the need for Croatian experiences while co-operation with Colombia opens up the possibility of other forms of political and economic cooperation as well as mutual exchanges of experiences and cooperation in other developing countries. The specific sectoral and geographical priorities as well as the principles of development, cooperation and humanitarian assistance have been listed so far. Furthermore, in order to achieve these priorities and to make our cooperation more effective, we have been guided by the following concrete objectives. In international donations and projects, we will promote interdependence of peace, security and development as a prerequisite for progress. We also want to promote the visibility and value of the experiences of new donors as well as the importance of our own experience in development policy, in which we will promote and use our own experience of democratic transition. We intend to strengthen political and project cooperation with partners in Croatia in particular with the economic sector, civil society, academic community and institutions and thus strengthen their knowledge and capacity to participate in development policy as well as withdraw funds from EU external instruments, which is also one of the most important objectives of the government's action. We consider it important to strengthen public awareness of the importance, advantages and our niche in development policy. Finally, we will work to further improve the legal, administrative and budgetary framework for a better and more effective implementation of the development cooperation and humanitarian assistance policy. This is what we want to make a further step towards the establishment of the Republic of Croatia as developed countries and donors with a clear vision that helps others and thus meets its own priorities. Finally, several concrete data on the current activities of the Republic of Croatia in international, development and humanitarian policy. Since 2011 since our exclusive donor, Croatian institutions have implemented over 1300 development and humanitarian projects. Of which, the Ministry of Foreign and European Affairs has implemented more than 80 projects. In BiH alone, Croatia has implemented more than 500 projects since 2011. In 2016, for example, Croatia allocated a total of 279.5 million kuna for official development aid, representing 0.08% of gross national income. It should be noted here that the EU as the largest and most important global donor has committed to total 0.7% of gross national income by 2030. We can see this as an opportunity to promote our own experiences and capacities through this form of cooperation, meet foreign policy priorities and strengthen our visibility in this policy. The greatest visibility and efficiency are achieved through direct bilateral projects in developing countries. Croatia thus built, renovated, trained and encouraged entrepreneurship in BiH's key partner, built schools and libraries and encouraged women's entrepreneurship in Afghanistan, treated children from Iraq, trained mine action in Colombia in Jordan, shared knowledge about the construction of sustainable tourism in Tunisia, taught doctors about the treatment of PTSD in Ukraine, and assisted in the pre-accession process in EU candidate countries. In addition, Croatia is an active partner in numerous multilateral projects and initiatives within the UN, the European Union and international organisations. Thus, the EU's Trust Fund for the Colombia is financially supported and support is being prepared for the Joint Fund for Africa, which is rooting for the issue of illegal migration. In this context, too, we are considering financial participation in other EU initiatives and funds such as the Economic Resistance Initiative and the European Sustainable Development Fund. Finally, Croatia regularly helps the needy population in numerous humanitarian crises around the world, for example in Macedonia and BiH, Iraq, Syria, etc. Finally, the National Development Cooperation Strategy for 2017 to 2021 is the result of successful cooperation between a number of bodies, NGOs, economic associations and scientific institutions. We therefore believe that this document will be a good guide and a basis for further development aid to be even more concentrated, more realistic, more efficient and visible in the international community. The plan is to focus our activities on better aligning economic and foreign policy objectives in the next period. Following the adoption of the National Strategy, the implementation programme will be adopted where we will focus on key projects in Bosnia and Herzegovina, Southeast Europe and other priority countries. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-HR_2017-10-25-0.u54361	I would like to thank Mr. President, Madam Minister with co-workers, colleagues and colleagues. The development aid strategy and humanitarian aid, although these are two different things, but both play an unequivocal role in profiling foreign policy, i.e. I think they are one of the most important and potentially effective instruments of foreign policy. They are often suppressed and neglected because they may not have a momentary effect, but in some medium-term frameworks they can potentially produce very positive effects for one country in a whole series of dimensions of economics that are most often mentioned which is of course very important, but also one that seems even more important to me, which is building an international reputation and the status of a country, building what is called the image of a country in some more modern language. And depending on the image especially when it comes to small countries, the country's influence in international organisations is changing significantly. This may seem amazing at first glance, but it is fundamentally changing how much the word of a country is worth in different multilateral and organisational organisations, as well as initiatives and projects. So, with one-time projects, even when the interests of the country are at stake. So, in order to defend your interests effectively, it's very important what reputation, image, what image you've created about yourself internationally. In this respect, development aid and humanitarian aid and the entire development aid project play a major role. The concept or the project and its objectives are also strongly reflected in internal policy. It should not be fooled that foreign policy is in general, and particularly in this dimension of development and humanitarian assistance, something that is completely separate or that has little to do with the country's internal policy and situation, it is reflected very strongly on how we see ourselves as a society. So, what kind of society or society we are in this case when it comes to concept, what kind of society we want to be, and through that are projected and the values that we represent as a society. ParlaMint-HR_2017-10-25-0.u54390	Thank you very much, Mr. Vice President. And you can be sure that the SDP club will talk about the topic ahead of us. Here I welcome once again the Deputy Prime Minister, Foreign Minister with Associates, and I would like to say on behalf of the SDP Club that we believe that this is the kind of document that a single country must have, in particular, an EU member. The document is well-assembled and we will support it. But, of course, I would also like to make a few comments on this strategy that is under discussion today, so first of all, let me say that development of cooperation is an important component of foreign policy and should contribute to the achievement of solidarity and justice in international relations and the resulting promotion in all societies of common values codified in the UN Charter. Now, if we ask ourselves what these values are, and it was in the debate, here's what the UN Charter can open, the first two articles are talking about exactly that. The main objective of development policy is to enable citizens of all countries to manage their own present and future for their own benefit and to enable each individual to make personal progress and development. In central development policy there is man, his dignity, his rights and his needs. Today, the differences between developed and undeveloped countries as well as inequalities within the countries themselves are particularly between the rich minority and the poor majority and cause various social deviations. From terrorism to non-accepting sustainable development, from opposition to climate change to organised crime, from corruption to deepening the digital gap. Development cooperation is an instrument for correcting these deviations in order to strengthen human dignity and stability of international relations. Unlike humanitarian aid, whose primary objective is to save lives in crisis situations and facilitate human suffering, the development of co-operation focuses on solving the cause of these problems and, after identifying the source of the problem, provides proposals to promote overall political, economic and social development with the ultimate goal of achieving its sustainability and training of society and individuals to manage their own destiny. The document that is right in front of us says that there has been a change of paradigm in development cooperation, and more than this whole story we call development aid than development cooperation because it has been realized that it is much better and more effective to achieve these objectives that I have mentioned. So it's no longer a unilateral relationship between donors and donors, but a two-way partnership relationship where donors as partners determine together with those who receive assistance needs that need to be met and defined by the country itself partner who is the recipient of such assistance. It should be stressed and this is very important given that Croatia is not a rich country and we cannot allocate large funds from the budget for development aid, that development cooperation has its own financial, but also non-financial element, so by transferring knowledge, experience or some other forms of non-financial assistance you can do much more and better to help countries that need help than just pumping money. Croatia thus launched a series of projects with Ukraine that we know where we transfer certain experiences from the peaceful reintegration of the Croatian Podunov. But we can also do so in neighbouring countries, as well as in those parts concerning meeting the conditions for EU accession. We talked about new stakeholders in development cooperation and should certainly be recognized by civil society, religious and scientific organisations, economies, businesses. I would also like to mention here that, for example, a form of non-financial development aid can be and should certainly be used. ParlaMint-HR_2017-10-25-0.u54419	Thank you. Dear Vice-President of the Croatian Parliament, distinguished MPs and MPs. The 2016 work report is the last report I present to the Croatian Parliament as president of the first call of the commission which acts as a permanent, independent and independent state body. It is therefore an opportunity to point out, in addition to the data relating to the past year, the overall results of the tasks that the Law on the Prevention of Conflicts of Interest put into the power of the Commission and the reasons for which the future amendments and addition of the laws governing the problem of conflict of interest need to be extended and the powers of the Commission itself should be strengthened. It is also necessary to point out the differences that require greater budgetary resources to be provided to the Commission in the coming period and to enable it to further develop and strengthen human, technical and other logistics capacities. In particular, when discussing this report on the Committee on the Constitution, Rules of Procedure and the political system, one of the respected members of that committee asked about how I assess the overall effects of our work on the basis of all the procedures carried out over the past years. Two important colossals are important to take care of when answering this question. The first track is a comparison of the situation found at the time of entry into office of the members of the current commission and the situation that this meeting of the commission leaves at the end of the term of office. The second is the assessment of the general situation whether progress has been made over the past five years in understanding what conflict of interest is and what these are the obligations of officials in the conduct of public duties, in particular in respect of respect of ethical principles of action. When we talk about the development of the commission as an institution, it is important to remind that during the period from the adoption of the first law in 2003 to the new law of 2011 the commission was set up as one of the working bodies of the Croatian Parliament. By doing so, it is understood that the members of the commission did not act professionally. Of all 11 members, 6 members were selected from the ranks of representatives in the Croatian Parliament and 5 from the ranks of prominent public employees. Members only gathered for discussion and decision-making at periodic sessions. Such a body in the nature of things couldn't even develop its own infrastructure. At the beginning of 2013, when members of the first permanent and professional meeting of the commission began their work. The commission was already physically separated from the premises of the Croatian Parliament in the space it was assigned to use by the then Agency for the Management of State Property, and in the Office of the Commission, i.e. the professional service employed a total of 8 employees. However, although the commission had already had certain technical equipment, i.e. with a certain number of computers, there were no adequate software information solutions to monitor work processes, and in particular in the conditions in which members of the Committee carry out their duties in a permanent working relationship and as such, they participate daily and directly in the performance of their work together with other employees employed in the office itself. Therefore, it was primarily necessary to design and develop work processes in the circumstances in which both members and officials in the Commission’s Office found themselves for the first time. In the previous reports, it was described that the situation found in early 2013 must also be understood through the fact that the circumstances, that the commission was not the owner of the programme through which the data from the submitted property reports were publicly published on the Commission’s website. Namely, the owner of the programme was APIS. Then there was no electronic system for the registration of e-mails and e-mails, there were no other registers and eyewitnesses necessary to monitor the status of individual items and time limits. There was no internal connection between individual computers, let alone the link between the information system of the Commission and the databases held by other state bodies. Finally, a register of officials is established with data on all officials who are required to comply with the provisions of the Law on the Prevention of Conflicts of Interest. Although the obligation to form a register of officials was prescribed in the first law of 2003, at the time of the entry into office of the current member of the Committee, the commission did not welcome the already completed list or catalogue of duties and officials covered by the Commission's jurisdiction. Among other things, the Croatian Parliament has never provided the Committee with a list of duties it appoints as officials within the meaning of the Law on the Prevention of Conflicts of Interest. The Commission therefore considers that the jurisdiction of law enforcement in itself implicitly includes the powers of its interpretation for the purposes of the application of this law only interpreted to whom all of this law applies within the meaning of Article 3(2) of this Act. These are also the duties of the Director-General of Croatian Television, members of the Management Boards of HANFA, HERA, a member of the Council for Electronic Media, a member of the Competition Protection Council and a number of others on the basis of an autonomous interpretation included in the register of officials, and their behaviourists are invited to submit a report on the property situation. Before the new 2011 Act, there was no obligation to carry out previous, or administrative checks, and no regular verification of the data from the reports submitted on the property situation for each report submitted. Nor was this verification carried out prior to the entry into office of the current Board of Committees. I would point out that there were no technical preconditions for the implementation of systematic, previous and regular checks for each report submitted in early 2013. After initial experience in the implementation of the Law on the Prevention of Conflicts of Interests in 2014, an information project was carried out to inform the overall work processes within which a new website has been developed, an electronic form of property reports and an office business system under which the electronic entries of the entry and exit mail are conducted, and electronic eyewitnesses for each particular area of work within the competence of the Committee. Within the framework of the office business system, individual items shall be automatically deployed and assigned to work. In the framework of the information project, an internal network has been established through which data and shared documentation are accessed according to different levels of authorisation in the Committee itself. For the first time, a register of officials was established. Further updates of the IT system were carried out in 2015 and 2016. The result of all investments made so far in the development of IT tools and applicative solutions has been recognised by relevant international bodies and organisations as an example of good practice in the verification of property reports. Due to the informative expenditure of innovation in the development of application-based IT solutions as well as the content of a number of resolved subjects, the Commission has been invited to present our system at relevant international conferences and to participate in other knowledge-sharing and practice-sharing projects, such as OECD, OSC, Regional Anti-Corruption Initiative. After the Commission established its own IT system in 2016, the merger of this information system was carried out with the databases of other state bodies, on the basis of which the Commission, for the purposes of verifying the data from the property report, can make direct insights into the data of, for example, the Tax Administration, Katastra, and in other public registers and registers, such as vehicle registers, vessels, aircraft registers, and data from the Central clearing company, the Commercial Court and others. By the end of this year, the final phase of the upgrade of the IT tools will be completed during the term of this call, which has developed a specific programme for the automatic comparison of data from submitted information with data available by other state bodies. It is a program which, using the given algorithms, analyses the results of the verification itself, comparing the reported and collected data and indicating the observed deviations. In particular, I point out that all information processes are integrated into an office business programme through which individual items are created and managed. Work assignments, enrolment and follow-up deadlines shall be assigned. These are important data and documentation stored on each activity carried out, which means that for each activity, a trace of who has it and when it has been carried out is stored. The content and amount of investments made in the established IT system describes how the Commission was built on the inside and how it gradually developed assumptions for effective and successful work as part of the available means. On the other hand, the way in which the Commission interpreted certain institutes prescribed by the provisions of the Law on the Prevention of Conflict of Interest and the overall practice in the procedures carried out by the Commission testifys to the construction of this institution in its relationship with other state bodies, in relation to officials, to the media and to the public in general. First of all, I would like to remind myself that the new Law on the Prevention of Conflict of Interests has been in force since 10 March 2011, and that the institutional implementation of this Act has been very short before the entry into force of the current Commission's call at the beginning of 2013. Since by disbanding the 6th meeting of the Croatian Parliament at the end of 2011, the previous meeting of the Committee lost the quorum necessary for decision-making, and the selection of members of the new Committee was not carried out throughout 2012. A situation which did not exist within a period of more than a year by the law was negatively affected by the perception of the officials of the obligations arising out of that law. Since neither the applicable law was in force in a systematic and consistent manner since it was implemented by the Commission which had no permanent, independent and independent state body, it was necessary to establish, first of all, a system which was sent that the obligations laid down in the provisions of this Act should be respected. In this respect, circumstances have imposed an obligation to initiate and implement a large number of procedures, since even the simplest and clearest rules and provisions were indeed high. All previous reports, as well as the 2016 Work Report, describe experience in the application of this law as well as a large number of problems arising from existing provisions and how the Commission found its answers and solutions. In fact, even though this is not about extensive law, each chapter carries its own obstacles and traps. A number of quite simple issues such as those of who is considered an official, within which time limits the Commission is authorised and obliged to initiate the procedure, who can be considered as an interesting person and what can be considered a business relationship and other similar issues, the law itself does not always give rise to a simple answer. Content of the interpretation of a number of concepts is only given through the practice of the Commission which then checked, and then mainly confirmed the competent administrative courts in connection with the lawsuits of officials against decisions which determine that there has been a violation of the law. In order to establish good practice in the conduct of procedures under the Commission's jurisdiction, continuity is necessary, and it really existed in the term of this call. Statistics in 2016 do not differ significantly more than previous years. In this respect, 40 public meetings were held where a total of 87 decisions on non-starting were taken, 71 decisions to initiate the procedure and 113 final decisions on conflict of interest of officials. Decisions laying down a violation of the law which may be imposed by that law of the prescribed sanctions in 10 cases have imposed a penalty and a total of 403 000 sanctions for the suspension of the net part of the monthly salary of officials. In addition, 181 cases were resolved concerning the applications submitted for the opinion of the Committee. I will mention that on today, the Committee has given almost 900 reasoned opinions and arguments. Some 300 decisions on the non-starting of the procedure and over 400 final decisions have been taken to impose approximately a million and 700 thousand kuna sanctions. Since the number of opinions given is double the number of cases in which the infringement of the law has been found, statistics prove that the Commission is primarily a preventive body. And given that less than 2,000 officials are in the commission's jurisdiction, this total number of completed proceedings is indeed impressive. The Commission's tasks can be roughly divided into three basic groups. These are the procedures relating to the submission and verification of the property report, the submission of an opinion at the request of the officials when they are in doubt how to act and then the conduct of the proceedings to determine whether the official is in conflict with the interests and whether he has committed a breach of the provisions of the law. In the cases of submission and verification, the most significant result of the work of the Commission is the establishment of a register of officials and electronic forms of the Property Report and the establishment of an information system for direct access to other national authorities' databases, in particular the development of an information programme that independently conducts an automatic comparison of the reported and collected data and analyses the results of the verification. In the cases of opinions given at the request of officials, the Commission has built a recognizable system of good practice in managing conflicts of interest and standards of conduct that are reasonably expected from officials. Among the cases in which the law was found to have been broken, two significantly different groups differ. The first type are cases where it is found that there has been an infringement of a certain administrative rule, whether it is a obligation, ban or a restriction for which the infringement is legally prescribed the possibility to impose sanctions. Other types are cases in which the Commission determines that in a particular situation there is some form of conflict of interest of officials or is determined that the official has violated the legal principles of action. In such decisions, however, the Commission does not impose sanctions on the official, because for a situation where there is a conflict of interest and for a situation where the official has not acted in accordance with ethical principles, the law did not prescribe the possibility of imposing sanctions. It is about declarative decisions which are actually an institutional and public condemnation of certain procedures or failures of officials. When making a general assessment of whether progress has been made over the past 5 years in understanding what conflict of interest is and in recognising the duties of officials in the conduct of public duties, and in particular in terms of the obligation to respect ethical principles, experience and practice may have special significance in the 2016 cases. Therefore, in the cases decided by the Commission in 2016 there are cases of highly-positioned officials in which the Commission found that the official was in a situation of potential conflict of interest and that by failing to provide full and timely information to the public, he violated the principles of action. These items from 2016 are certainly neither the first nor the only items of high-ranking officials decided by this committee's assembly. Yes, of course. Among those who have been sanctioned by the Commission for the established infringement of an administrative rule or who have established a breach of the principle of action are a number of members of both previous and previous governments as well as a number of other officials of all levels and all policy options. However, the 2016 items show both officials and the public, and all of us together as a society must continue to develop awareness of the importance of timely recognition of controversial or potentially controversial circumstances and of the importance of proper management of situations of conflict of interest and of the correct distinction of such situations from those in which the official took the next step and thus fell into the sphere of punitive corruption. In decisions in which the Commission has established the existence of a conflict of interest, the Commission has repeatedly called for interpretations of the Constitutional Court of the Republic of Croatia's decision of 7 November 2012 which clearly points out that the conflict of interest, and thus the potential conflict of interest itself is not disputed. It may even be obtained independently of the will of the official himself, following the mere fact that some of the circumstances of his private life are intertwined with powers or obligations in the conduct of a particular public duty. What can be disputed is the way the official managed such a situation or did not manage it at all. Depending on the circumstances of a particular case, if the official fails to do in due time everything necessary to separate his private interests from the public in such a situation, there may be an initiation of any of the prohibited activities or in a difficult case and the initiation of any of the corrupt offences for which the jurisdiction of the commission generally ceases and the jurisdiction of the law enforcement authority begins. As pointed out by the Constitutional Court, the Commission is acting in the preventive sphere of the fight against corruption, therefore institutes prescribed by the provisions of this law necessarily interpret in the spirit of the general purpose of its adoption, which is to prevent private influences on the decision-making process by the public authority, strengthen the integrity, objectivity and impartiality and transparency in the conduct of public duties and strengthen citizens' confidence in public authorities. The conflict of interest exists in all situations where private interest affects or may be considered or may potentially affect impartiality in decision-making. It is resolved by removing or exempting from actions that may sometimes require full distance from the whole process and even from accompanying discussions. The public declaration of the circumstances that compromise the participation of officials in decision-making by establishing objective and impartial criteria on the basis of which the decision will be taken and by increasing transparency of both the official and the body in which the public is acting. The greater the circumstances that compromise the participation of officials in certain processes, the greater the transparency obligations that enable public or citizens to be convinced of the way officials act. When the Constitutional Court of the Republic of Croatia abolished certain powers of the Commission which could possibly be considered strong in one part of the reasoning of the decision, it also called for the jurisprudence of international courts which found that the way officials acted was a matter of general public interest. As one of the principles of action, it is also prescribed by our law that officials are personally responsible for the body or citizens they have chosen or appointed. The experience in the resolved cases has led to the fact that by denying the existence of circumstances that may constitute conflict of interest, by seeking transparency in the work of the officials and the body in which he behaves, the conflict of interest itself will not disappear. These are precisely the absence of a timely and appropriate reaction and the transparent publication of all those data and documentation on the circumstances in which the public has the right to know, officials have violated their own integrity and credibility as well as citizens' trust. In this respect, neither after all the procedures carried out nor from 2016, it can be concluded that all officials and in all situations have developed awareness of the importance of removing any suspicion that public duty has been used to achieve private interests at the expense of the public. On the other hand, the overall number of requests submitted for the opinion and the Commission's guidance shows that part of the officials recognises that trust in public action is the highest capital and that in the external impression they leave with their own actions, they take account. All strategic documents relating to the prevention and prevention of corruption highlight the importance of preventive action and, in the context of this preventive action, the building of ethical principles and values. Indeed, respect for ethical principles and standards of good practice are the best prevention. The tasks of preventive bodies, of course including the commission, shall necessarily include the development of public awareness and accountability, the implementation of educations both by the official and the public. Among other things, this is also achieved by making declarative decisions describing specific situations in which there is a conflict of interest and an action or failure of an official that constitutes an infringement of one of the prescribed ethical principles. In order to succeed in establishing sound preventive mechanisms, however, it is important that no subject in which these circumstances are being reconsidered should be predicated, for example, in such a way that public space is given meaning or weight as if it were a corrupt criminal offence. On the other hand, the purpose of the law cannot be realised even if a particular case remains almost undetected or does not produce any reaction. The Commission, in all cases, has access in the same way and cannot influence the public interest or the way in which a particular case will be reported in the media. Experience shows that the consequences resulting from a particular case sometimes depend largely on this echo in public and political life rather than on the content itself and the severity of a particular case. I mentioned earlier that trust is the highest capital in public action. And that capital is not only important for a single official but also for the public authority itself. I believe that with my approach to work, the way decisions are made, as well as the content of the reasons for these decisions, the commission has been built as an institution that is trusted. And that is the result that obligates the members of the current assembly in the remaining few months until the end of the term of office as well as any subsequent assembly of whoever its member is. In the past 5 years, good foundations have been established in each of the areas of work. Given that this first assembly began almost from scratch, now that significant progress has been made in the development of the institution in the future must also be expected exponentially more. But to succeed better than the one who will leave this behind, it is necessary to improve the legislative framework and provide the commission with greater budgetary resources. It is necessary to extend the circle of officials to all duties with which corruption risk is naturally linked. The Commission’s procedures should clearly define the powers or actions to be taken by the Commission, which must comply with the laws laid down by supervisory authorities in the process of verifying the data contained in the reports submitted. The sanctioning system must also be effective in relation to those officials or former officials who no longer exercise their salary or public duties compensation. The deadlines of action as well as the deadlines of limitation must be clearly defined. Obligations, prohibitions and restrictions during the term of office and, in particular after the term of office, must be the result of a fair balance between the protection of public interest and the legitimate private interests of the official himself. For certain duties, specific rules or exceptions may be laid down to the general rules which are in accordance with the nature of the transaction or with the duties performed by that official. It is necessary to reduce or limit the number of exceptions allowed during the term of office. It is also necessary to sign up for the strengthening of the restrictions on gender relations with legal persons or with those bodies in respect to which the official has the possibility of influence when it comes to business entities owned or owned by members of his family. The implementation of a systematic check of the property report for each report delivered necessarily requires the strengthening of the human capacity of the Commission's budget. Namely, the information tools are only one analytical aid and a basis that facilitates and accelerates certain processes but for the results of the automatic analysis obtained, it is also necessary to carry out an administrative procedure that either justifys a identified discrepancies or takes steps to determine that there has been a breach of the law and that requires new people in the commission who will be well trained to do so. Also relevant international initiatives involving the Commission as well as the obligation to monitor trends and new knowledge in the field of conflicts of interest and to monitor international jurisprudence require strengthening of human capacities and for the purpose of international cooperation. The commission is currently located in an area which does not allow the reception of new employees or employees into the Commission's office. In addition to the above, the problem of paying for the city of Zagreb's claims based on the acquisition without the basis of the use of space after a solution has been abolished which was returned to the ownership of the Republic of Croatia. In view of the above, budget resources as well as adequate space for the Commission to expand and further institutional development and growth are necessary for the upcoming period. Following all this, I hope the Croatian Parliament accepts this report. Thank you.

ParlaMint-HR_2017-10-25-0.u54517	Dear Mr. President of the Croatian Parliament, Madam President of the Committee for Decision on the conflict of interest with members, colleagues and colleagues. In front of us is the 2016 Commission Report, which will be supported by the Club of Representatives of the Most, as he did with an earlier report. This is practically the final report of the current composition of the commission. We are about to have a procedure to elect a new commission, and therefore this report is not just a report on last year's work but can be seen as a summary of the work of this commission over the past five years. The old Law on the Prevention of Conflict of Interests, which was adopted in 2003, was in force until the adoption of this existing law in 2011, entrusted the law to an old commission which was then drawn up from the ranks of MPs and some respected public employees. However, this new law of 2011 has prescribed the establishment of a Commission to decide on conflict of interest as a permanent, independent and independent state body performing the tasks of its work and competence. And of course, what is important by law is explicitly forbidden any form of influence on the work of the commission that could endanger its independence and independence in making decisions from its work. Of course, in many cases the new law with the new composition of the commission has also contributed to a different picture of work and positive evaluations of the commission's work over the past few years. However, let us remind ourselves that although the law was passed in 2011 the commission was not elected immediately afterwards. It was only in February 2013 that the commission began to work. This shows the severe paternity of the commission, which, together with the legal framework, was, in my opinion, the result of the European negotiators' demands at the time in the process of joining the European Union. At the time, I was an external member of the Committee on the Constitution, Rules of Procedure and the political system at the time of the adoption of this law, and I am well aware of the context of its adoption. There was then a lot of EU insistence on a series of provisions that did not have most EU member states in their regulatory legal framework at all. The decision of the Constitutional Court of the Republic of Croatia in November 2012 which abolished several provisions in almost 10 articles of the existing law had an important impact on the scope of the Commission's powers and today, I would say, a law which lacks certain mechanisms in which the commission would fully make the relevant roles to be played. However, the fact is that the law is almost not changed except in a few smaller provisions, which is not really the responsibility of the commission, but of this Croatian Parliament. Because the Constitutional Court has abolished the important provisions that should have allowed the Commission to determine the facts of its own actions, that banking and other financial institutions are obliged without delay at the request of the Commission to deliver the requested notices and evidence. Provisions resulting from the Commission’s verification procedure have also been abolished to establish a discrepancy or disproportionateness in the property of officials, etc. What should certainly be pointed out in the work of the commission in recent years is, in particular, their work on the publication of the report on the property status of officials, on the establishment of the register of officials and what I would point out to the most important, and that was the most important thing about the work of the commission, it is a proactive preventive action. That is exactly what the commission considers to be one of its basic, even most important tasks. The text of the report also says that it is proactively so that during the days of interpretation and instructions, standards of good practice are built and developed, and clear instructions and expectations are drawn up from those who perform public duties. It was in that part that the commission had to act frequently through the interpretation of the law with its opinions and decisions and in that part, even in today’s debate, that was the most reprehensible. Because individual decisions, some opinions differed in terms of individual officials. But the fact is that, in our opinion, this role of the commission was inevitable because of a relatively cold law that, in its entire formulation, never really began to work in the work of the commission that actually started working after the Constitutional Court's decision. And this commission has never received a new whole-to-do legislative framework. It was forced to actually just create one good practice. In this, we can say that there were also wrong decisions and that there were decisions that each individual representative in this House would disagree personally. But in our opinion, the commission was doing the right thing regardless of individual decisions. And in any case, we and the Representatives' Club support such an approach to the commission that has created a solid practice that will certainly be important in the work of every future composition of this committee. Over the past five years, the commission has initiated hundreds of procedures for violating certain provisions of the law by officials. Mostly due to injuries in the delivery of the property report. Furthermore, Article 5, which prescribes the principles of action of officials, in particular in the part where they must act with honor, honest and responsibleness and where their public duty must not be used for the personal breakthrough or breach of a person associated with them. Article 14 concerning membership in administrative bodies and supervisory committees and Article 7 relating to the prohibited activities of officials. The fact is that the commission is not an anti-corruption committee, so it does not determine the corruption of officials. There are no instruments for this, especially after the Constitutional Court's decision, unlike, for example, Slovenia which has a Commission to prevent corruption. In its report, the Commission provides certain examples from 2016 which it considers important. What we would like to point out in this report is that, in fact, all these decisions and this debate that the commission actually had to have by making its decisions and opinions in the proceedings that were later conducted before the Administrative Courts, that is, one decision before the Constitutional Court. In fact, we have a dialogue between the commission and the courts that needs to contribute to a better understanding of the Institute of Conflict of Interest and the interpretation of the Law on the work of the future commission. Of course, in the decisions of the commission, there can also be some who are later challenged and which has been respected by the Administrative Court of Appeal, but of course it is inevitable in the work of each body. But in any case, it should be said that the commission and it is visible from the report in a large number of these situations were right, and it is not in some cases that it is not. Over the past 5 years, the commission has managed in our opinion to create a significant decision-making base and opinions that will certainly affect the work of each future commission. In this regard, in the opinion of the Most Representatives Club, the Committee on Conflict of Interest has fully confirmed its existence as an important, independent and impartial body contributing to the transparency of public action of officials. And without an individual decision that we do not always have to agree with, the commission carried out its tasks impartially and independently, which is exactly the most important thing for the work of this body and its perception in the public. And it may be important to say, because there are countless reports of various bodies that we receive in this Croatian Assembly. This is a report by the body that actually completes its first five-year term and should be said and it is never enough to repeat that the Commission on the decision of conflict of interest is a good story which has succeeded in contrast to perhaps some of the other bodies that have proved themselves in practice and in this sense our positive assessment of this report should also be a guideline for the work of each new composition regardless of the individual decisions that individual officials will not agree with. At the end of what is important to us, the Croatian Parliament has not essentially changed the legislative framework on which the Committee on Conflict of Interest would act. And that's what we consider a problem. Because it's been left with the fur law, there's been plenty of room left for the commission to kind of proactively interpret the law. That's what some of you pointed out in the debate that it wasn't the best solution. But it is primarily the responsibility of the Croatian Parliament. We are just given the opportunity to allow the new commission to be elected soon to have one better quality legislative framework in which they will not have to interpret or seek the meaning of the legal provisions, but will have a better quality arrangement in which they will be more clearly prescribed and for officials what their behaviour is expected to be. And that there would be no debate as it was today that officials sometimes did not know what they were forbidden to do or act as officials and what their obligations were in accordance with the law. ParlaMint-HR_2017-10-25-0.u54609	Thank you, Vice President of the Croatian Parliament. First of all, on behalf of the Commission, I would like to thank all the committee clubs for supporting the Commission's report this year as well as all the previous ones. There's been a lot of issues here today. Somehow, I have an instinctive urge and a need to address them in particular all the issues that have been put into the context of possible political pressures, but this really is neither the place nor the time as well as the issues of a possible choice or re-election of new members. But what was also the most talked about, and these are the necessary amendments and additions to the law, so a question arose whose government had a longer period of opportunity to make the necessary interventions in this law. I would like to point out that the fact that no changes have been made so far has occurred and is not anyone's responsibility. The Commission also said at the beginning of its term of office that, in fact, for quality changes, quality well thought-out and well-prepared solutions would need to be allowed to apply the law sufficiently long and sufficiently extensively in practice and that relevant interpretations and reviews of other competent authorities such as administrative courts in the proceedings in which committee decisions were being reviewed when officials applied to them. This report as well as all previous ones should have actually served as a catalogue for you MPs to, together with the officials, because they refer to this law as seeing what the problems arising from this law are in practice. But I think we got it all wrong in that part, and I can see that there's been a lot of talk about it today that the commission was arbitrarily interpreting something that wasn't properly dealt with in the law. It's not really like that. So when I spoke about the fact that the circle of officials was not regulated to this extent that at the beginning of the term of office we had a full catalogue of duties and of the officials to whom it relates, I meant, among other things, the provision of Article 3(2) which states that officials and those appointed as such by the Croatian Parliament and that the Croatian Assembly never provided the list of those people who should be covered by the competence of the commission, so the commission as the body responsible for the implementation of the law also interpreted whom such provision would apply, so that group was counted by the director of HRT, members of the administrative boards of regulatory agencies and similar. It's the part where the commission interpreted the provisions of the law but not the way out of what the law says. I also see that, even after almost 5 years of working this committee, there is still a fundamental misunderstanding of the difference between the concept of what it is a conflict of interest and what administrative rules are. Although it is precisely that part of the practice that the Commission has put forth the most effort and effort. So conflict of interest, now that I do not repeat the definition of the law is that situation when someone is compromised due to some circumstances from their private life in the decision-making process. It does not have a time-limited duration sequence, it really represents something where public interest is expected to remove its participation in the decision-making process. This has nothing to do with other administrative rules that are purely formal in nature, such as the rule that officials cannot be members of the management and supervisory boards of commercial companies, both during the term of office as regards official Zoran Milanovic, whom you mentioned and those officials who have suspended the mandate but are bound by the same ban for the next 12 months. So it is not on the commission to assess whether this prohibition is justified or not. We've heard more than once today from SDP and HNS representatives that they find this rule illogical. Today, an article has been published in the media, I do not know if this is a credible or authentic data but it seems that the Government is not satisfied with the interpretation of the commission of this provision in relation to the appointment of Mr Rosandic to a member of a single governing body, so the management of a company in majority state ownership. These are not situations where in any way there is a space of discretion in the application of this law by the Commission. These are clear rules that are what they are, if they need to be changed and in what way they cannot decide and not decide the commission, but this body. As regards the data and documentation collected by the Commission for the purposes of carrying out the procedure under its jurisdiction, one of the questions raised was why the commission in one of the cases of the local official requested documentation relating to the amendments and additions to the spatial plan of the arrangement. I don't know what case it is right now, but I guess that's why it's very likely that these particles included a property owned by an official or a member of his family. This is highly part of the work and competence of the commission to determine whether, within those powers it has, the official has used public duties for personal breach or not. If not, such a situation will also be clearly seen from the collected documentation. The law itself says that even an official can ask the commission, it is the only situation where the commission has to carry out the procedure, when the official himself asks, because it is possible that he was attacked by political opponents or the media, that he is in a situation of conflict of interest and that perhaps that's not the case. If nothing is disputed, the Commission will, on the basis of such a prepared case and documentation, take one reasoned, over-advised, reasoned decision to establish that there are no elements to initiate the procedure because there is no knowledge that the official has committed the provision of the provisions of this law or a decision on the non-starting of the procedure will be taken. As I have stated so far, there have been 300 such decisions, and I think they are very much protecting both the integrity and credibility of the official himself and the confidence of the citizens in the bodies of the public government, and I see no reason why any official in situations that did not fear the Commission's demands would be provided with the necessary documentation to make objective, impartial, well-founded and reasoned decisions. I would also like to point out that when we draw up each of our reports, we point to all those irregularities of the law which, on the one hand, are not sufficiently protected by public interest, but some of them are not in favour of the officials themselves, and they should. In this context, the decision concerning the first Deputy Prime Minister last year was particularly apostrophysed, so not in the context of the decision itself, but in the context of what the competent administrative court said in the procedure of control of the commission's decision. According to the Court of Justice, one of the shortcomings in the law is that the decisions of the Commission, in which a breach of the principle or where a situation of conflict of interest has been found, cannot be brought to the Court of Justice on the basis of provisions of the Law on the prevention of conflicts of interest. So for now, this is the right and only decision because, in fact, so far, there has been one single lawsuit by officials against such a declarative decision of the commission, and then one single word of the competent administrative court that said that there is no law on the prevention of conflicts of interest, there is no possibility of filing an administrative action on these declarative decisions, that officials cannot be denied the possibility of a legal remedy. So actually the Court of Justice, based on interpretation of the provisions of the other laws, allowed the suit in that case. This case is therefore apostrophealed precisely for the reason that, when amending and supplementing the Law on the prevention of conflicts of interest and in relation to these declaratory decisions, it would provide for a clear possibility of filing an administrative action or another form of legal remedy against the decisions of the Commission, unless the official is in agreement with it. In the end, I would like to say that no law, however high-quality it is, can define and prescribe everything. So, every body that implements a single law will have to be involved in interpreting the provisions of that law, what is said legal terms... So we are within our work and competences, this is not about arbitration or extending our powers, but about, for example, when you have a notion, which is a business relationship, then you should interpret, for example, whether it can be considered as a business relationship and a contract on the lease of business space, which is not explicitly stated in the law itself. [[The speaker doesn't understand.]] The Commission interpreted that such a situation must also be considered a business relationship, because it has a corrupt risk, because one side gives money, the other side acquires business space. And such an interpretation was confirmed by the first-degree administrative court, but also by the High Administrative Court, which actually means that there was no discretion of the commission here. Finally, I believe that indeed, after this relaxation, therefore, we are now talking about a total of 900 opinions, 400 final decisions that have been found to violate the law, 300 decisions on the non-starting of the procedure, and we have enough quality material on which a new law can be made, respecting all that the Commission has said and in the need to spread its own powers, but also, it would be good to involve officials themselves in this public debate. Because completely different problems, for example, have officials who perform their duties at local or regional level compared to those who, for example, perform others, as they consider public duties, such as the head of extra- budget funds, public institutions and by accident. I hope that the work of this committee in this Croatian Assembly will be of great help. Thank you.
