ParlaMint-HR_2017-10-25-0.u54633	Thank you very much, well-respected Vice-President of the Croatian Parliament, respected MPs and MPs. For starters, before I start talking about the report in a few sentences, I'd like to paint you a picture of the reality facing the citizens in 2016. If we were to move the entire population of the Republic of Croatia to this assembly with 151 MPs and MPs, although unfortunately there are a lot of you here tonight, a lot less than that would look like this. Your total 12 would live in extreme poverty with less than 1300 kuna per month for all living expenses and 11 would be blocked because of your debts. You 14 would not be able to afford heating in winter and for five, the only source of water would be wells, wells and local water supply, not public water supply. 31 among you would be a victim of discrimination, and most of you would not seek protection. If you were all retired 11 you'd be receiving a pension of less than 500 kuna. If you were all students, only 15 would have a place in a dorm, multiple cheaper than an apartment. These and numerous other data are presented in the 2016 Ombudsman, who in over 50 thematic chapters represents a mirror of everyday life in Croatia, especially the most vulnerable citizens, the poor, the socially endangered, the elderly, those with health problems and who live in rural areas, members of national minorities, persons deprived of freedoms and others. In addition to the analysis and assessment of the human rights situation in Croatia, the report also contains 220 recommendations to competent authorities aimed at more strong protection of human rights and combating discrimination in Croatia and we also give an assessment of the extent to which the data we know and available competent authorities have met the recommendations of the 2015 report. This report was prepared on the basis of information from several sources, primarily 5433 cases in which we handled during 2016, which is the largest number since the founding of the Ombudsman's institution 25 years ago and 86% more than in 2012. A large majority of these are complaints from citizens and for the first time in 2016 they were most discriminatory on all grounds and in all areas of life while the judiciary fell to second place by number of complaints. This is probably for the sake of our better availability primarily through the district offices in Osijek, Rijeka and Split but partly because we are more public field tours. In addition to these items, the report has also been prepared on the basis of information we asked for more than 300 addresses from state institutions, cities and municipalities, civil society associations, religious communities, trade unions, social partners, universities, public companies and many others, so over 300 recipients and we used data collected during field work because in 2016 we were in more than 50 cities and municipalities throughout Croatia. Finally, information is also a source of relevant national and international research. And as the Vice-President of the Croatian Parliament pointed out, it was drawn up in accordance with the Law on the Ombudsman, the Law on Discrimination of Discrimination and the Law on the National Prevention Mechanism. With regard to cooperation with competent authorities, both in the preparation of the report and throughout the year, especially in the work on individual cases, I would particularly like to highlight the good cooperation we have with the Ministry of Demography, family, youth and social policy, the Ministry of the Interior and the Ministry of Justice. While the Ministry of State Property, the Ministry of Construction and Spatial Planning and the University of Zagreb are among those who respond to us irregularly or only after more haste. We are certainly trying to improve cooperation with stakeholders and citizens through stronger information on human rights issues and the protection of human rights and the prevention of discrimination, news that we send to more than 700 email addresses across the country. As regards the state of human rights and the occurrence of discrimination in Croatia, several problems have been identified in 2016. It is primarily a difficult social situation in the country, then inequality before the law and the great mistrust that citizens have in institutions at all levels. Unequal rural development, impatience and lack of dialogue in public space as well as tensions aimed at national minorities and questioning their rights. We also pointed to these problems in the years prior to 2016, and for 2016 there was further specific and stagnation of public policies since the legislative activity was extremely modest and that there was no work on strategic documents. And unfortunately, during 2016, we also witnessed pressures on independent institutions such as the Electronic Media Council and civil society organisations. A large number of areas in all areas, a large number of problems in all areas of life stem from the fact that citizens are in an extremely difficult social and economic situation because almost 20% of them live at risk of poverty. As a result, it is necessary to strengthen the social care system, including the increase in the amount of the guaranteed minimum fee, which currently amounts to only 800 kuna for a single person. And set the same base for all social benefits. The supervision of social service providers should also be more effective since it is now carried out by only 5 senior inspectors over 4700 service providers. In addition, Croatia still does not have a social housing strategy that would include all socially vulnerable groups: homeless people, families living in extremely unfavourable housing conditions, young people coming out of alternative care, victims of violence, citizens affected by extreme situations. On the other hand, I welcome the significant change, the positive change announced in the Government's opinion on this report, which is the introduction of a social criterion for granting subsidies for the energy recovery of households as part of the fight against energy poverty. When we talk about local level, unfortunately, although all cities and municipalities have a legal obligation for people receiving guaranteed minimum fees to pay business costs and large cities to provide them with social housing. Part of them does not, because they either do not have the means or simply do not see it as one of the priorities which is another indicator of the inequality of socially endangered citizens before the law and of unequal regional development. Most of the problems are more pronounced in rural areas that include more than 90% of Croatia's surface area. While at the level of Croatia as a whole, the risk of poverty is 20%, in Brod-Posavina and Virovitica-Podravska County, the risk is more than 33%. And that's why it's not surprising to move into big cities, to other countries. In order to prevent it, it is necessary to make rural areas more transport-related throughout the year and to provide citizens with access to public services, and to involve units of local and regional self-government in making decisions concerning them. Part of the recommendations we have made on this subject have already been adopted. A Strategy for Regional Development has been adopted, and expert support for local and regional experts in the preparation of ESI projects that need to be used to a much greater extent. Among the particularly vulnerable groups that often live in rural areas and in major poverty are elderly people, and Croatia is the fourth country in the EU to be poor. 94000 pensioners receive a pension of less than 500 kuna, and some are hard to retire because part of the pensioners are paid only through banks, which is particularly difficult to hit those in whose places there are no banks. There is no adequate support mechanisms for family members who care for their old, and those who live alone are not sufficiently available services in the community such as home assistance, nor are they sufficiently informed of the risks that result in the conclusion of a life support contract. These life support contracts pose a serious risk to the economic abuse of older people even with tragic consequences, and no protection mechanisms have yet been prescribed to prevent such abuses. It is a positive announcement that their conclusion will be prohibited for persons providing social services and members of their families, but it is also necessary to introduce a register of these contracts and limit their number by their supporter. It is also extremely important because it is about citizens who do not have time, that judicial proceedings for their dispersion are urgent. As far as the problem with performances is concerned, more than 320,000 citizens are in a blockade, 110 thousand of them due to debts that do not exceed 10.000 kuna. Partial amendments to the Implementing Law have certainly come in handy, but not enough. The performance needs to restore its true meaning, and this is the payment of outstanding basic debt with the necessary costs of the procedure, rather than those which often exceed only the basic debt. It is also necessary to increase the ability to collect the debt before the execution, and the perpetrator must be timely and truly informed about it so that he can use his procedural rights and settle the debt with as little interest and expenses as possible. A particular problem is the demand collection agencies which are excessively aggressive and endanger the privacy and dignity of the debtors, and for their business, standards and rules of conduct are not prescribed for their work. As a result of all this, the exercise became a synonym for injustice, inequality and inadequacy of citizens, which perhaps best illustrates the complaint received in the family of poor property that had to be represented itself in the executive procedure after their request for free legal assistance was denied in the Office of State Administration, after they had not received a reply after the multiple expiry of the deadline on the application from the Ministry of Justice. In court proceedings, they represented themselves as the legal ignorant party, and the bank was represented by a lawyer. In such a case, the same procedural position of the parties and the equality of the weapons before the court cannot be spoken of. And of course this family was slow to lose and had to move out of the house. This also tells a lot about the system of free legal aid which is part of the right to a fair trial and in a situation where almost 20% of people at risk of poverty are extremely important to be quality, effective and to ensure real equality before the law to those who cannot afford legal aid. A large role in this system is played by associations authorised to provide BPP. However, in 2016 they were allocated half as much as the year before, and yet small funds arrive only in mid-November and are not evenly deployed regionally, which is why the BPP system is largely inefficient. When we talk about the whole area of justice, it remains burdened with long-term procedures and unequal court practice which violates legal certainty and citizens' trust. According to the World Bank, 59% of citizens believe they cannot expect a fair trial before Croatian courts. However, rather than raising the budget of the Judicial Academy by 72% in the last five years, and it is also necessary to raise confidence in the work of the State's Attorney's Office in better communication with citizens who continue to complain that they cannot get an answer, i.e. information on the state of the case or the response to a presentation from the State's Attorney's Office. The citizens also addressed us in 2016 because of problems in the field of working and official relations. Many remain in fear of losing their jobs to almost anything employers ask them to or deny them as the failure to pay overtime. This, with unemployment, is certainly one of the biggest reasons for evicting citizens of all age groups, all degrees of education, and so will likely remain until employees can afford a decent life for themselves and their families. A step forward would certainly be to strengthen the capacity of inspection services in both the Ministry of Labour and the pension system and the Ministry of Administration. As for complaints about official relations, they still come from police officers the most. In 2016, most often due to the performance of second place of work under the written orders of the manager. When we talk about discrimination on work and employment, we have also established in the restructuring of companies, mainly on the basis of age and health conditions. In one of the cases in which we have found discrimination in a large company, it has revised unfavourable contracts after our conduct, introduced a programme of training measures for better paid jobs and education of equal-treatment executives, but how frequent this is practice, it also says that as many as 40% of the workers on the Technological Higher lists are those over 50 years old. For many students of vocational training, 2016 marked changes in travel costs. So those who do not have access to adequate public transport, mainly from rural areas, have lost the right to this compensation which has led them to an unequal position compared to others. Also due to the benefits that employers achieve in the conclusion of a training contract, in practice, the most felt by those over 29 years of age who do not have a professional training measure is almost gone. As for the area of health and here we see inequalities. In 2016, waiting lists for certain checks and procedures have been further increased, making those who cannot pay the costs of a private doctor in a much more unfavourable position. Long waiting lists suit private clinics and for this it is particularly important to set rules and boundaries between these two systems. Public health as strategic and non-profit social activities based on the solidarity of the healthy with the sick and private which is governed by market rules. Part of the problem certainly causes the system's information decoupling but not the patient's arrival on the agreed terms. And in such a situation, citizens are not provided with a timely approach or the principle of equal and accessible access to health services has been brought to life. And that is why it is extremely important to announce to the government in the opinion of our report that the Ministry of Health will, in accordance with the recommendation by the end of the year, adopt a regulation on ensuring medically acceptable time to achieve the health care measures. The specific topic is the availability of health services in rural areas and in islands or in traffic isolated places and is also related to health and environmental protection in which we receive more and more complaints from year to year. As for complaints about discrimination, we received a total of 387 which is 36% more than in 2015, and the most numerous are on the basis of ethnicity, faith and age and in the field of labour and employment, public information and the media of the administration. However, this number does not reflect the actual frequency of discrimination because it is still not reported most often. According to the 2016 survey, 20% of citizens have been discriminated against once in the last 5 years, but as many as 68% have asked for no protection because they think that nothing would change or that their situation would even deteriorate which is an extremely strong indicator of mistrust in institutions. Finally, almost 40% of citizens view discrimination as the biggest or one of the biggest problems in Croatian society and it is more devastating that we still do not have a national plan to combat discrimination. A third of the complaints we take into account discrimination relate to the basics of race and national origin. And there is a growing number of court proceedings that cited ethnic hatred as a motive for discrimination, especially affecting Roma and Serbs. EU Agency for Fundamental Rights data show that almost 93% of Roma have incomes lower than the poverty line and only 8% are considered employees. Those who work most often are employed in public works and many Roma families continue to live in extremely poor conditions, without bathroom and toilets, in containers, in the woods, without access to drinking water, without electricity. In order to integrate them into society, other than, of course, improving living conditions, it is necessary to ensure access to education including for adults directly linked to their involvement in the labour market. Hate crimes, motivated by hatred of national origin, are most often directed towards members of the Serb national minority and are not rarely equalised collectively with those responsible for the Homeland War. They also face difficulties in various areas, official and public use of languages and letters, representations in bodies in which they are legally guaranteed and access to means of public communication. Those living in areas that were occupied except for poverty are also struggling with problems caused by unrenewed infrastructure and the particular problem is that there is not a complete process of electrifying the village that had power connections before the war. Insufficient developments remain in the area of reconstruction and housing. Members of national minorities are often exposed to impatience that negatively affects the exercise of minority rights and to a number of incidents of unacceptable and discriminatory speech and interethnic relations. But the rights of national minorities are not and must not be an act of mercy of the majority people, but are the responsibility of a state that must ensure their respect. Through the prism, the initiative to remove guaranteed seats should also be looked at for representatives of national minorities in the Croatian Parliament, according to which they would be elected from the general electoral lists. Animosity has also been directed towards political inexperiences, journalists who have also been exposed to physical violence as well as activists. One of the reasons is that there is also a lack of dialogue in public space between both public and citizens, especially on the Internet. Freedom of expression is often interpreted as a right to speech without responsibility for what is wrong because it can indeed have serious consequences. And in that sense, a special responsibility lies right on you politicians because your words are being transmitted to the public and they reach out to a larger number of people, which is why they have a special weight. On the other hand, politicians rarely identify criticism they receive with hate speech. But by acting in the political arena, everyone must be prepared to be under increased fire by political opposition, and according to the European Court of Human Rights, this includes sharp, sharp and even criticism involving insult, provocation and satire. Of course, unstructured and unargumented statements most speak of those who refer them, but therefore there is also a mechanism of judicial protection, which every individual can claim for himself. When we talk about communicating with the symbols, I can only regrettably conclude that we are still witnessing the relativation of the Ustasha greeting "For Home Ready" although it is clearly illegal and sanctioned for violations against public order and peace, the Law on Discrimination of Discrimination and the Law on Public Assembly. There is a very clear case law and a very clear case law of the Constitutional Court. Police regularly file criminal charges for its use, courts consistently sanction it, but the ministry in administrative proceedings ignores the legal framework. This deepens the uncertainty, leaves the impression that the use of this greeting, this sign, is legal, and is not. Furthermore, the law has not yet been passed, although this was announced since 2004. which would regulate the setting of monuments and other features in public spaces. Including tombstones and monuments, which would not offend anyone, national, religious or moral feelings, such as those of the dead Serb paramilitary troops in the local cemetery in Borovo Selo. I am left with a brief look at the national preventive mechanism, i.e. the areas of persons deprived of freedom and those who are limited to freedom of movement. Even this year, we have not established actions that can represent torture or conditions that can represent torture, but we are humiliating, even inhumane. The prison system continues to record a reduction in overcrowding, although in some institutions capacity has crossed 130%, and a significant problem remains the health care of prisoners. According to information from the government's opinion on our report, according to the recommendation, systematiced posts in criminal authorities will be filled, and this should prevent situations where prisoners miss medical examinations, as there are no one to take them, but also increase the quality of the implementation of mandatory treatment measures on addiction. As for police treatment, it remains one of the most common causes of complaints against citizens, especially in situations where physical strength is used. And this year, and in 2016, we were also referred to by older people. The team is more concerned by the fact that an effective system of civil surveillance over police work has not yet been established. Terms of accommodation in part of psychiatric departments continue to endanger privacy and make it difficult to treat and rehabilitate. A particularly difficult situation, in which case, we found ourselves in a psychiatric hospital for children and youth. And inadmissible practice has also been observed in homes for older and powerless, including accommodation without the express consent of an elderly person or disrespecting their privacy, and in this system the problem is the lack of employees, which affects the quality of the service, which is particularly worrying in which there is no effective supervision of social service providers, which I was talking about earlier. The number of international protection seekers increased by 957% compared to 2015 until the capacity of the MUP changed, resulting in increased number and long-term demand. It is difficult to involve refugees and migrants in the education system and the labour market, which brings them into a vicious circle of economic and social marginalisation. And in this regard, it is particularly worrying that Croatia does not have a comprehensive integration policy, but only one aimed at searchers for international protection and no migration policy has been adopted. In December 2016, the first complaints of migrants in irregular situations were recorded, which claimed that they had been returned from Croatia to Serbia without individual procedures, and were instructed by the police to use violence. The MAP has dismissed all charges, but we continue to deal with this case, especially in view of the need to conduct an effective investigation which can only show whether there were irregularities. In this regard, I also point out the European Court of Human Rights rulings against Spain last week, which states that deterring persons at the EU's external borders is illegal and contrary to the European Convention on Human Rights. After all these problems I have mentioned and many others mentioned in the report, it is once again necessary to say that in the report that lies before you, a total of 220 recommendations to the competent authorities. And this is the contribution of the Advocate General's office to achieving long-term, sustainable and effective solutions. Also, as the authority of the Croatian Parliament for Human Rights, I invite you to use this report and especially recommendations in your daily work and for the benefit of citizens. In addition, the implementation of the recommendations depends on the Croatian Parliament, which is also mentioned among other things that the recommendations of the 2014 report have been implemented to a much greater extent than those for 2015. Citizens on the other hand are increasingly reporting their problems, and there has been an increase of 86% of the number of cases in the last 4 years of our work. I also want to thank them for their trust, encourage them to continue to do so, because this is the only way they can affect change for the better. I am taking the opportunity to highlight the very important role that cooperation plays. Of course, all the constructive proposals and remarks we receive are valuable to us, both from you MPs and from other stakeholders, because they in the far line can make the report even better. And of course, the government's opinion on a report that we understand as a continuation of our dialogue. And finally, I would like to remind you that in May, right in this hall and together with the gathering, a really large number of stakeholders from the country and abroad, we have marked 25 years since the establishment of the institution of the Police Attorney, which at that time grew into a really strong institution and developed from its narrow... the roles of an independent national human rights institution with an internationally recognised highest degree of independence in the central anti-discrimination body and the national preventive mechanism.
